**Antisemitism**,[^cite_note-2] or **Jew-hatred**,[^cite_note-3] is hostility to, prejudice towards, or discrimination against [Jews](25955086.md). A person who harbours it is called an **antisemite**.[^cite_note-Oxford2-4][^cite_note-MWdef-5][^cite_note-Lewis_Antisem06-6] Whether antisemitism is considered a form of [racism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racism) depends on the school of thought.[^cite_note-7][^cite_note-8][^cite_note-9] Antisemitic tendencies may be motivated primarily by negative sentiment towards [Jews as a people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_peoplehood) or negative sentiment towards Jews with regard to [Judaism](15624.md). In the former case, usually known as [racial antisemitism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_antisemitism), a person's hostility is driven by the belief that Jews constitute a distinct race with inherent traits or characteristics that are repulsive or inferior to the preferred traits or characteristics within that person's society.[^cite_note-10] In the latter case, known as [religious antisemitism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_antisemitism), a person's hostility is driven by their religion's perception of Jews and Judaism, typically encompassing doctrines of supersession that expect or demand that Jews turn away from Judaism and submit to the religion presenting itself as Judaism's successor faith—this is a common theme within the other [Abrahamic religions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abrahamic_religions).[^cite_note-11][^cite_note-12] The development of racial and religious antisemitism has historically been encouraged by [anti-Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Judaism),[^cite_note-13][^cite_note-14] which is distinct from antisemitism itself.[^cite_note-15]

There are various ways in which antisemitism is manifested, ranging in the level of severity of [Jewish persecution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_Jews). On the more subtle end, it consists of expressions of hatred or discrimination against individual Jews and may or may not be accompanied by violence. At the extreme, it consists of [pogroms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pogrom) or [genocide](12441.md), which may or may not be state-sponsored. Although the term *antisemitism* did not come into common usage until the 19th century, it is also applied to previous and later anti-Jewish incidents.[^cite_note-16] Historically, most of the world's violent antisemitic events have taken place in Europe, where modern antisemitism began to emerge from [antisemitism in Christian communities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antisemitism_in_Christianity) during the Middle Ages. Since the early 20th century, there has been a sharp rise in [antisemitic incidents across the Arab world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antisemitism_in_the_Arab_world), largely due to the advent of [Arab antisemitic conspiracy theories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theories_in_the_Arab_world), which were influenced by [European antisemitic conspiracy theories](16938566.md).[^cite_note-Herf_2009-17][^cite_note-JCPA_2020-18]

Beginning in 1879,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBein1990595-19] the compound word *Antisemitismus* was first used in print in [Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Germany) as a "scientific-sounding term" for *Judenhass* (lit. 'Jew-hatred'),[^cite_note-20] and it has since been used to refer to anti-Jewish sentiment alone.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELipstadt201922–25-21][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJohnson1987133-22][^cite_note-JustJews-23] In the 21st century, the idea that there is a variation of antisemitism known as "[new antisemitism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_antisemitism)" has been discussed. According to this view, since [Israel](9282173.md) is a [Jewish state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_state), expressions of [anti-Zionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Zionist) positions could harbour antisemitic sentiments, and [criticism of Israel](30074296.md) can serve as a vehicle for attacks against Jews in general.[^cite_note-24][^cite_note-25][^cite_note-JP-26]

## Origin and usage

### Etymology



1879 statute of the Antisemitic League

First used in the 1770s by members of the [Göttingen school of history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G%C3%B6ttingen_school_of_history), the coining of "[Semites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semitic_people)" as [biblical terminology for race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biblical_terminology_for_race) was derived from *[Shem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shem)* (שֵׁם), the name of one of the three [sons of Noah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sons_of_Noah) in the [Book of Genesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book_of_Genesis),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaasten200357–73-27] together with the parallel terms *[Hamites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamites)* and *[Japhetites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japhetites)*.

The origin of "antisemitic" terminologies is found in the responses of Orientalist [Moritz Steinschneider](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moritz_Steinschneider) to the views of Orientalist [Ernest Renan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernest_Renan). Historian [Alex Bein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alex_Bein) writes: "The compound *anti-Semitism* appears to have been used first by Steinschneider, who challenged Renan on account of his 'anti-Semitic prejudices' \[i.e., his derogation of the Semites as a [race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Race_\(human_categorization\))]."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBein1990\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidcQOn0y8ENg4CpgPA594_594\]-28] Psychologist [Avner Falk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avner_Falk) similarly writes: "The German word *antisemitisch* was first used in 1860 by the Austrian Jewish scholar Moritz Steinschneider (1816–1907) in the phrase *antisemitische Vorurteile* ('antisemitic prejudices'). Steinschneider used this phrase to characterise the French philosopher Ernest Renan's false ideas about how '[Semitic races](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semitic_Race)' were inferior to '[Aryan races](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aryan_race)'".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFalk200821-29]

[Pseudoscientific](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pseudoscience) theories [concerning race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_racism), civilization, and "progress" had become quite widespread in Europe in the second half of the 19th century, especially as [Prussian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prussia) nationalistic historian [Heinrich von Treitschke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heinrich_von_Treitschke) did much to promote this form of racism. He coined the phrase "the Jews are our misfortune", which would later be widely used by [Nazis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazism).[^cite_note-30] According to Falk, Treitschke uses the term "Semitic" almost synonymously with "Jewish", in contrast to Renan's use of it to refer to a whole range of peoples,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFalk2008\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidVWL4ja2BbnECpgPA21_21\]-31] based generally on linguistic criteria.[^cite_note-32]

According to philologist [Jonathan M. Hess](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jonathan_M._Hess), the term was originally used by its authors to "stress the radical difference between their own 'antisemitism' and earlier forms of antagonism toward Jews and Judaism".[^cite_note-Hess-33]



Cover page of Marr's *The Way to Victory of Germanicism over Judaism*, 1880 edition

In 1879, German journalist [Wilhelm Marr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilhelm_Marr) published a pamphlet, *Der Sieg des Judenthums über das Germanenthum. Vom nicht confessionellen Standpunkt aus betrachtet* (*The Victory of the Jewish Spirit over the Germanic Spirit. Observed from a non-religious perspective*), in which he used the word *Semitismus* interchangeably with the word *Judentum* to denote both "Jewry" (the Jews as a collective) and "Jewishness" (the quality of being Jewish, or the Jewish spirit).[^cite_note-34][^cite_note-35][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELevy2010123–129-36] He accused the Jews of a worldwide conspiracy against non-Jews, called for resistance against "this foreign power", and claimed that "there will not be a single office in the land, including the highest, which will not have been usurped by the Jews".[^cite_note-37]

This followed his 1862 book *Der Judenspiegel* (*A Mirror to the Jews*), in which he argued that "Judaism must cease to exist if humanity is to commence", demanding that Judaism be both dissolved as a "religious-denominational sect" and subject to criticism "as a race, a civil and social entity".[^cite_note-WM-38][^cite_note-39] In the introductions to the first through fourth editions of "*Der Judenspiegel*", Marr denied that he intended to preach Jew-hatred and claimed he instead intended to help "the Jews reach their full human potential", which he said could happen only "through the downfall of Judaism, a phenomenon that negates everything purely human and noble".[^cite_note-WM-38]

This use of *Semitismus* was followed by a coining of *[Antisemitismus](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/Antisemitismus)*, which was used to indicate opposition to the Jews as a people[^cite_note-40][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams2024286-41] and opposition to the Jewish spirit, which Marr interpreted as infiltrating German culture.

The pamphlet became very popular, and in the same year Marr founded the *Antisemiten-Liga* ('League of Antisemites'),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZimmermann1987\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidtYW013SjKM4CpgPA71_71\]-42][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETroy2024391-43][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELevy2010123–129-36] apparently named to follow the *Anti-Kanzler-Liga* ('Anti-Chancellor League').[^cite_note-MZ1987-44] The league was the first German organisation committed specifically to combating the alleged threat to Germany and German culture posed by the Jews and their influence and advocating their [forced removal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_transfer) from the country.

So far as can be ascertained, the word was first widely printed in 1881, when Marr published *Zwanglose Antisemitische Hefte*, and [Wilhelm Scherer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilhelm_Scherer) used the term *Antisemiten* in the January issue of *[Neue Freie Presse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neue_Freie_Presse)*.[^cite_note-45][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELevy2010123–129-36]

The *[Jewish Encyclopedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_Encyclopedia)* reports, "In February 1881, a correspondent of the *[Allgemeine Zeitung des Judentums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allgemeine_Zeitung_des_Judentums)* speaks of 'Anti-Semitism' as a designation which recently came into use ('Allg. Zeit. d. Jud.' 1881, p. 138). On 19 July 1882, the editor says, 'This quite recent Anti-Semitism is hardly three years old.'"[^cite_note-DeutschG-46]

The word *antisemitism* was borrowed into English from German in 1881. *[Oxford English Dictionary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxford_English_Dictionary)* editor [James Murray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Murray_\(lexicographer\)) wrote that it was not included in the first edition because "*Anti-Semite* and its family were then probably very new in English use, and not thought likely to be more than passing nonce-words... Would that anti-Semitism had had no more than a fleeting interest!"[^cite_note-47][^cite_note-48] The related term *[philosemitism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philosemitism)* was used by 1881.[^cite_note-philosemitism-49]

### Usage

From the outset the term *anti-Semitism* bore special racial connotations and meant specifically prejudice against [Jews](25955086.md).[^cite_note-MWdef-5][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELipstadt201922–25-21][^cite_note-JustJews-23] The word *Semitic* was coined for use in linguistics by German Orientalist [August Ludwig von Schlözer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/August_Ludwig_von_Schl%C3%B6zer) in 1781 to designate the [Semitic group of languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semitic_languages)—[Aramaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aramaic), [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic), [Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language) and others—allegedly spoken by the descendants of the Biblical figure [Shem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shem), son of [Noah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noah).[^cite_note-Vermeulen_2015_p._252-50][^cite_note-51]

### Definition

Though the general definition of *antisemitism* is 'hostility or prejudice against Jews', and, according to [Olaf Blaschke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olaf_Blaschke), the word has become an "umbrella term for negative stereotypes about Jews",[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWeinberg2010\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid0HDeEPouQm0CpgPA18_18\]-52] a number of authorities have developed more formal definitions.[^cite_note-USDS-53]

Writing in 1987, Holocaust scholar and [City University of New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_University_of_New_York) professor [Helen Fein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helen_Fein) defined it as "a persisting latent structure of hostile beliefs towards Jews as a collective manifested in individuals as attitudes, and in culture as myth, ideology, folklore and imagery, and in actions—social or legal discrimination, political mobilization against the Jews, and collective or state violence—which results in and/or is designed to distance, displace, or destroy Jews as Jews."[^cite_note-54]

Elaborating on Fein's definition, Dietz Bering of the [University of Cologne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Cologne) writes that, to antisemites, "Jews are not only partially but totally bad by nature, that is, their bad traits are incorrigible. Because of this bad nature: (1) Jews have to be seen not as individuals but as a collective. (2) Jews remain essentially alien in the surrounding societies. (3) Jews bring disaster on their 'host societies' or on the whole world, they are doing it secretly, therefore the anti-Semites feel obliged to unmask the conspiratorial, bad Jewish character."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFalk2008\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidzL_0WOiZj0oCpgPA5_5\]-55]

For Swiss historian Sonja Weinberg, as distinct from economic and religious [anti-Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Judaism), antisemitism in its specifically modern form shows conceptual innovation, a resort to "science" to defend itself, new functional forms, and organisational differences. It was anti-liberal, racialist and nationalist. It promoted the myth that [Jews conspired to 'judaise' the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Protocols_of_the_Elders_of_Zion); it served to consolidate social identity; it channeled dissatisfactions among victims of the capitalist system; and it was used as a conservative cultural code to fight emancipation and liberalism.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWeinberg2010\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid0HDeEPouQm0CpgPA18_18–19\]-56]



A caricature by [Charles Lucien Léandre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Lucien_L%C3%A9andre) (France, 1898) showing [Rothschild](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rothschild_family) with the world in his hands

In 2003, Israeli politician [Natan Sharansky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natan_Sharansky) developed [what he called the "three D" test](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Three_Ds_of_antisemitism) to distinguish antisemitism from criticism of Israel, giving [Delegitimization](40229800.md), Demonization, and Double standards as a litmus test for the former.[^cite_note-57]

British-American historian [Bernard Lewis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernard_Lewis), writing in 2006, defined antisemitism as a special case of prejudice, hatred, or persecution directed against people who are in some way different from the rest. According to Lewis, antisemitism is marked by two distinct features: Jews are judged according to a standard different from that applied to others, and they are accused of "cosmic evil".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELindemannLevy20108-58] Thus, "it is perfectly possible to hate and even to persecute Jews without necessarily being anti-Semitic" unless this hatred or persecution displays one of the two features specific to antisemitism.[^cite_note-Lewis_Antisem06-6]

There have been a number of efforts by international and governmental bodies to define antisemitism formally. In 2005, the [United States Department of State](31975.md) stated that "while there is no universally accepted definition, there is a generally clear understanding of what the term encompasses." For the purposes of its 2005 Report on Global Anti-Semitism, the term was considered to mean "hatred toward Jews—individually and as a group—that can be attributed to the Jewish religion and/or ethnicity."[^cite_note-USDS-53]

In 2005, the European Monitoring Centre on Racism and [Xenophobia](34141.md) (EUMC, now the [Fundamental Rights Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fundamental_Rights_Agency)), an agency of the [European Union](9317.md), developed a more detailed [working definition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EUMC_Working_Definition_of_Antisemitism), which stated: "Antisemitism is a certain perception of Jews, which may be expressed as hatred toward Jews. Rhetorical and physical manifestations of antisemitism are directed toward Jewish or non-Jewish individuals and/or their property, toward Jewish community institutions and religious facilities." It also adds that "such manifestations could also target the state of Israel, conceived as a Jewish collectivity," but that "criticism of Israel similar to that leveled against any other country cannot be regarded as antisemitic."[^cite_note-antisemitic-59] It provided contemporary examples of ways in which antisemitism may manifest itself, including promoting the harming of Jews in the name of an ideology or religion; promoting negative stereotypes of Jews; holding Jews collectively responsible for the actions of an individual Jewish person or group; [denying the Holocaust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holocaust_denial) or accusing Jews or Israel of exaggerating it; and accusing Jews of [dual loyalty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual_loyalty) or a greater allegiance to Israel than their own country. It also lists ways in which attacking Israel could be antisemitic, and states that denying the Jewish people their right to self-determination, e.g., by claiming that the existence of a state of Israel is a racist endeavor, can be a manifestation of antisemitism—as can applying double standards by requiring of Israel a behavior not expected or demanded of any other democratic nation, or holding Jews collectively responsible for the actions of the State of Israel.[^cite_note-antisemitic-59]



1889 Paris, France elections poster for self-described "*candidat antisémite*" [Adolphe Willette](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adolphe_Willette): "The Jews are a different race, hostile to our own... Judaism, there is the enemy!" (See file for complete translation.)

The EUMC working definition was adopted by the [European Parliament](9581.md) Working Group on Antisemitism in 2010,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaizels202314–15-60][^cite_note-61] by the [United States Department of State](31975.md) in 2017,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaizels202315–16-62][^cite_note-63] in the Operational Hate Crime Guidance of the UK [College of Policing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_of_Policing) in 2014[^cite_note-64] and by the UK's [Campaign Against Antisemitism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campaign_Against_Antisemitism).[^cite_note-65] In 2016, the working definition was adopted by the [International Holocaust Remembrance Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Holocaust_Remembrance_Alliance).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaizels202315–17-66][^cite_note-67][^cite_note-68] IHRA's [Working definition of antisemitism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Working_definition_of_antisemitism) is among the most controversial documents related to opposition to antisemitism, and critics argue that it has been used to censor criticism of Israel.[^cite_note-69] In response to the perceived lack of clarity in the IHRA definition, two new definitions of antisemitism were published in 2021, the [Nexus Document](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nexus_Document) in February 2021 and the [Jerusalem Declaration on Antisemitism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerusalem_Declaration_on_Antisemitism) in March 2021.[^cite_note-70]

### Evolution of usage

In 1879, [Wilhelm Marr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilhelm_Marr) founded the Antisemiten-Liga (Anti-Semitic League).[^cite_note-71] Identification with antisemitism and as an antisemite was politically advantageous in Europe during the late 19th century. For example, [Karl Lueger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karl_Lueger), the popular mayor of [fin de siècle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fin_de_si%C3%A8cle) [Vienna](55866.md), skillfully exploited antisemitism as a way of channeling public discontent to his political advantage.[^cite_note-Karl-72] In its 1910 obituary of Lueger, *[The New York Times](30680.md)* notes that Lueger was "Chairman of the Christian Social Union of the Parliament and of the Anti-Semitic Union of the Diet of Lower Austria.[^cite_note-73] In 1895, [A. C. Cuza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A._C._Cuza) organized the *Alianța Antisemită* and the *Liga Antisemită Universală* in Bucharest.[^cite_note-74] In the period before [World War II](32927.md), when animosity towards Jews was far more commonplace,[^cite_note-75][^cite_note-76] it was not uncommon for a person, an organization, or a political party to self-identify as an antisemite or antisemitic.[^cite_note-77][^cite_note-78]

&#x20;The early [Zionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zionist) pioneer [Leon Pinsker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leon_Pinsker), a professional physician, preferred the clinical-sounding term ***Judeophobia*** (also ***Judaeophobia***) to antisemitism, which he regarded as a misnomer. The word *Judeophobia* first appeared in his pamphlet *[Auto-Emancipation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auto-Emancipation)*, published anonymously in German in September 1882, where it was described as an irrational fear or hatred of Jews.[^cite_note-79] According to Pinsker, this irrational fear was an inherited predisposition.[^cite_note-Bartlett2005-80]

> Judeophobia is a form of demonopathy, with the distinction that the Jewish ghost has become known to the whole race of mankind, not merely to certain races... Judeophobia is a psychic disorder. As a psychic disorder, it is hereditary, and as a disease transmitted for two thousand years it is incurable... Thus have Judaism and Jew-hatred passed through history for centuries as inseparable companions... Having analyzed Judeophobia as a hereditary form of demonopathy, peculiar to the human race, and represented Jew-hatred as based upon an inherited aberration of the human mind, we must draw the important conclusion, that we must give up contending against these hostile impulses, just as we give up contending against every other inherited predisposition.[^cite_note-81]

In the aftermath of the [Kristallnacht](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kristallnacht) pogrom in 1938, German propaganda minister [Goebbels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Goebbels) announced: "The German people is anti-Semitic. It has no desire to have its rights restricted or to be provoked in the future by parasites of the Jewish race."[^cite_note-82]

After 1945 [victory of the Allies over Nazi Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/End_of_World_War_II_in_Europe), and particularly after the full extent of the [Nazi genocide against the Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Holocaust) became known, the term *antisemitism* acquired [pejorative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pejorative) connotations. This marked a full circle shift in usage, from an era just decades earlier when "Jew" was used as a pejorative term.[^cite_note-83][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBein1990580-84] Yehuda Bauer wrote in 1984: "There are no anti-Semites in the world ... Nobody says, 'I am anti-Semitic.' You cannot, after Hitler. The word has gone out of fashion."[^cite_note-85]

The term has been described as confusing, for in modern usage *Semitic* designates a language group, not a race. In this sense, the term is a misnomer, since there are many speakers of [Semitic languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semitic_languages) (e.g., [Arabs](2185.md), [Ethiopians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethiopian_Semitic_languages), and [Assyrians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyrian_people)) who are not the objects of antisemitic prejudices, while there are many Jews who do not speak [Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language), a Semitic language. Though *antisemitism* could be construed as [prejudice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prejudice) against people who speak Semitic languages, this is not how the term is commonly used.[^cite_note-86]

Characterizations of "Semite" as having little to no value on the socially constructed racial spectrum[^cite_note-87][^cite_note-Malina2021-88][^cite_note-Gannon2016-89] combined with its overuse due to popularization stemming from pro-Caucasian racism, as identification with antisemitism and as an antisemite was politically advantageous in Europe at least during the late 19th century, as previously discussed,[^cite_note-Karl-72] thereby diluting [anti-Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Judaism), have been made since at least the 1930s.[^cite_note-Sevenster1975-90][^cite_note-MZ1987-44]

In 1973, Bernard Lewis wrote that "the Semite \[... was] strictly linguistic in origin and use" and the idea that anything could be intuited beyond hatred of Jews through the use of the root word *[Semite](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/Semite)*, such as by *[The Economist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Economist)* and acclaimed biographer [Isaac Deutscher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isaac_Deutscher), was an effective [etymological fallacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Etymological_fallacy), arguing "on the contrary \[to the idea that t]he Nazis\['], who may be accepted as the most authoritative exponents of anti-Semitism, hostility was limited to Jews only, and did not include the other so-called Semitic peoples\[, they] found and still find no difficulty in simultaneously hating Jews and courting Arabs \[...]."[^cite_note-JustJews-23]

The term may be spelled with or without a hyphen (*antisemitism* or *anti-Semitism*). Many scholars and institutions favor the unhyphenated form.[^cite_note-IHRA2-1][^cite_note-91]

Identifying the operative [referent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Referent) in the original usage of *antisemitism*, referring to an action, state, condition, or doctrine and using the noun-creating suffix *[-ism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/-ism)*, to be *Semite*, and without addressing the scientific value of said term, in 1989, Shmuel Almog argued, "If you use the hyphenated form, you consider the words 'Semitism', 'Semite', 'Semitic' as meaningful ... \[I]n antisemitic parlance, 'Semites' really stands for Jews, just that."[^cite_note-92] In 2002, Padraic O'Hare, professor of Religious and Theological Studies and Director of the Center for the Study of Jewish–Christian–Muslim Relations at [Merrimack College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merrimack_College); and historians [Yehuda Bauer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yehuda_Bauer) and [James Carroll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Carroll_\(author\)). According to Carroll, who first cites O'Hare and Bauer on "the existence of something called 'Semitism'", "the hyphenated word thus reflects the bipolarity that is at the heart of the problem of antisemitism".[^cite_note-93] In 2003, [Emil Fackenheim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emil_Fackenheim) supported the unhyphenated spelling, in order to "\[dispel] the notion that there is an entity 'Semitism' which 'anti-Semitism' opposes."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPragerTelushkin2003\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidVK0llzUqQ2YCpgPA199_199\]-94] Others endorsing an unhyphenated term for the same reason include the [International Holocaust Remembrance Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Holocaust_Remembrance_Alliance);[^cite_note-IHRA2-1] historian [Deborah Lipstadt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deborah_Lipstadt);[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELipstadt201922–25-21] Conversely, in 2000, philologist Jonathan M. Hess identified the operative referent in the original usage to have been "antisemit\[e]".[^cite_note-Hess-33]

The [Associated Press](18935732.md) and its accompanying *[AP Stylebook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AP_Stylebook)* adopted the unhyphenated spelling in 2021.[^cite_note-95] Style guides for *[The New York Times](30680.md)* and *[Wall Street Journal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wall_Street_Journal)* later adopted this spelling as well.[^cite_note-96][^cite_note-97] It has also been adopted by many [Holocaust museums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holocaust_museums), such as the [United States Holocaust Memorial Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Holocaust_Memorial_Museum) and [Yad Vashem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yad_Vashem).[^cite_note-98]

### Eternalism–contextualism debate

The study of antisemitism has become politically controversial because of differing interpretations of the Holocaust and the [Israeli–Palestinian conflict](46216.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJudaken20181123–1124-99] There are two competing views of antisemitism—eternalism and contextualism.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConsonni202225-100] The eternalist view sees antisemitism as separate from other forms of racism and prejudice and an exceptionalist, transhistorical force [teleologically](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teleological) culminating in the Holocaust.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConsonni202225-100][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJudaken20181123,_1130-101] [Hannah Arendt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hannah_Arendt) criticized this approach, writing that it provoked "the uncomfortable question: 'Why the Jews of all people?' ... with the question begging reply: Eternal hostility."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJudaken20181130-102] Zionist thinkers and antisemites draw different conclusions from what they perceive as the eternal hatred of Jews; according to antisemites, it proves the inferiority of Jews, while for Zionists it means that Jews need their own state as a refuge.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJudaken20181135-103][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEUry20181151-104] Most Zionists do not believe that antisemitism can be combatted with education or other means.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJudaken20181135-103]

The contextual approach treats antisemitism as a type of racism and focuses on the historical context in which hatred of Jews emerges.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConsonni202227-105] Some contextualists restrict the use of "antisemitism" to refer exclusively to the era of modern racism, treating anti-Judaism as a separate phenomenon.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJudaken20181132-106] Historian [David Engel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Engel_\(historian\)) has challenged the project to define antisemitism, arguing that it essentializes Jewish history as one of persecution and discrimination.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConsonni202226-107] Engel argues that the term "antisemitism" is not useful in historical analysis because it implies that there are links between anti-Jewish prejudices expressed in different contexts, without evidence of such a connection.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJudaken20181130-102]

## Manifestations



Jews (identified by the mandatory [Jewish badge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_badge) and [Jewish hat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_hat)) being burned

Antisemitism manifests itself in a variety of ways. [René König](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ren%C3%A9_K%C3%B6nig) mentions social antisemitism, economic antisemitism, religious antisemitism, and political antisemitism as examples. König points out that these different forms demonstrate that the "origins of anti-Semitic prejudices are rooted in different historical periods." König asserts that differences in the chronology of different antisemitic prejudices and the irregular distribution of such prejudices over different segments of the population create "serious difficulties in the definition of the different kinds of anti-Semitism."[^cite_note-108]

These difficulties may contribute to the existence of different taxonomies that have been developed to categorize the forms of antisemitism. The forms identified are substantially the same; it is primarily the number of forms and their definitions that differ. [Bernard Lazare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernard_Lazare), writing in the 1890s, identified three forms of antisemitism: [Christian antisemitism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_and_antisemitism), economic antisemitism, and ethnologic antisemitism.[^cite_note-109] [William Brustein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_I._Brustein) names four categories: religious, racial, economic, and political.[^cite_note-110] The [Roman Catholic](606848.md) historian [Edward Flannery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_Flannery) distinguished four varieties of antisemitism:[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFlannery1985\[\[Category\:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_July_2022\]\]<sup_class="noprint_Inline-Template_"_style="white-space\:nowrap;">\[<i>\[\[Wikipedia\:Citing_sources|<span_title="This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears. (July_2022)">page needed</span>\]\]</i>\]</sup>-111]

- Political and economic antisemitism, giving as examples [Cicero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cicero)

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFlannery198516-112]

  &#x20;and [Charles Lindbergh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Lindbergh);

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFlannery1985260-113]

- [Theological or religious antisemitism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_antisemitism), also called "traditional antisemitism"

  [^cite_note-114]

  &#x20;and sometimes known as [anti-Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Judaism);

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFlannery1985289-115]

- Nationalistic antisemitism, citing [Voltaire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voltaire) and other [Enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Enlightenment) thinkers, who attacked Jews for supposedly having certain characteristics, such as greed and arrogance, and for observing customs such as [kashrut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashrut) and [Shabbat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shabbat);

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFlannery1985176-116]

- [Racial antisemitism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_antisemitism), with its extreme form resulting in [the Holocaust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Holocaust) by the [Nazis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi).

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFlannery1985179-117]

> [Europe has blamed the Jews](16938566.md) for an encyclopedia of [sins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sin).\
> [The Church](606848.md) blamed the Jews for [killing Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_deicide); [Voltaire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voltaire) blamed the Jews for [inventing Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_Christian). In the febrile minds of anti-Semites, Jews were [usurers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Usury#Judaism) and [well-poisoners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Well_poisoning#Medieval_accusations_against_Jews) and [spreaders of disease](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_Jews_during_the_Black_Death). Jews were the creators of both [communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_Bolshevism) and [capitalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_antisemitism); they were [clannish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_quarter_\(diaspora\)) but also [cosmopolitan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rootless_cosmopolitan); cowardly and warmongering; self-righteous moralists and defilers of culture.\
> Ideologues and demagogues of many permutations have understood the Jews to be a singularly malevolent force standing between the world and its perfection.

— [Jeffrey Goldberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeffrey_Goldberg), 2015.

[^cite_note-118]

[Louis Harap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_Harap), writing in the 1980s, separated "economic antisemitism" and merges "political" and "nationalistic" antisemitism into "ideological antisemitism". Harap also adds a category of "social antisemitism".[^cite_note-119]

- Religious (Jew as Christ-killer),

- Economic (Jew as banker, usurer, money-obsessed),

- Social (Jew as social inferior, "pushy", vulgar, therefore excluded from personal contact),

- Racist (Jews as an inferior "race"),

- Ideological (Jews regarded as subversive or revolutionary),

- Cultural (Jews regarded as undermining the moral and structural fiber of civilization).

### Religious antisemitism

Main article: [Religious antisemitism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_antisemitism)

See also: [Anti-Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Judaism), [Antisemitism in Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antisemitism_in_Christianity), and [Antisemitism in Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antisemitism_in_Islam)



The execution of [Mariana de Carabajal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francisca_Nu%C3%B1ez_de_Carabajal) (converted Jew), accused of a relapse into Judaism, [Mexico City](18987.md), 1601

[Religious antisemitism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_antisemitism), also known as [anti-Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Judaism), is antipathy towards Jews because of their perceived religious beliefs. In theory, antisemitism and attacks against individual Jews would stop if Jews stopped practicing Judaism or changed their public faith, especially by [conversion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_conversion) to the official or right religion. However, in some cases, discrimination continues after conversion, as in the case of *[marranos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marrano)* (Christianized Jews in Spain and Portugal) in the late 15th century and 16th century, who were suspected of secretly practising Judaism or Jewish customs.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFlannery1985135–141-120]

Although the origins of antisemitism are rooted in the Judeo-Christian conflict, other forms of antisemitism have developed in modern times. Frederick Schweitzer asserts that "most scholars ignore the Christian foundation on which the modern antisemitic edifice rests and invoke political antisemitism, cultural antisemitism, racism or racial antisemitism, economic antisemitism, and the like."[^cite_note-121] William Nicholls draws a distinction between religious antisemitism and modern antisemitism based on racial or ethnic grounds: "The dividing line was the possibility of effective conversion \[...] a Jew ceased to be a Jew upon [baptism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptism)." From the perspective of racial antisemitism, however, "the assimilated Jew was still a Jew, even after baptism.\[...] From the [Enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Enlightenment) onward, it is no longer possible to draw clear lines of distinction between religious and racial forms of hostility towards Jews\[...] Once Jews have been emancipated and secular thinking makes its appearance, without leaving behind the old Christian hostility towards Jews, the new term antisemitism becomes almost unavoidable, even before explicitly racist doctrines appear."[^cite_note-122]

Some Christians such as the Catholic priest [Ernest Jouin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernest_Jouin), who published the first French translation of *[The Protocols of the Elders of Zion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Protocols_of_the_Elders_of_Zion)*, combined religious and racial antisemitism, as in his statement that "From the triple viewpoint of race, of nationality, and of religion, the Jew has become the enemy of humanity."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMichael2008171-123] The virulent antisemitism of [Édouard Drumont](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89douard_Drumont), one of the most widely read Catholic writers in France during the Dreyfus Affair, likewise combined religious and racial antisemitism.[^cite_note-124][^cite_note-125][^cite_note-126] Drumont founded the [Antisemitic League of France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antisemitic_League_of_France).

### Economic antisemitism

Main article: [Economic antisemitism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_antisemitism)

Man kissing feet of another man with hooked nose, dropping money on his head

A World War II-era [Slovak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antisemitism_in_Slovakia) propaganda poster exhorts readers not to "be a servant to the Jew".

The underlying premise of economic antisemitism is that Jews perform harmful economic activities or that economic activities become harmful when they are performed by Jews.[^cite_note-MeyerBrenner220-127]

Linking Jews and money underpins the most damaging and lasting [antisemitic canards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antisemitic_canard).[^cite_note-128] Antisemites claim that Jews control the world finances, a theory promoted in the fraudulent *[The Protocols of the Elders of Zion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Protocols_of_the_Elders_of_Zion)* and later repeated by [Henry Ford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Ford) and his *[The Dearborn Independent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Dearborn_Independent)*. In the modern era, such myths continue to be spread in books such as *[The Secret Relationship Between Blacks and Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Secret_Relationship_Between_Blacks_and_Jews)* published by the [Nation of Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nation_of_Islam) and on the internet.

[Derek Penslar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Derek_Penslar) writes that there are two components to the financial canards:[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPenslar20015-129]

a) Jews are savages that "are temperamentally incapable of performing honest labor"

b) Jews are "leaders of a financial cabal seeking world domination"

[Abraham Foxman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham_Foxman) describes six facets of the financial canards:

1. All Jews are wealthy

   [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFoxman201084-130]

2. Jews are stingy and greedy

   [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFoxman201089-131]

3. Powerful Jews control the business world

   [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFoxman201093-132]

4. Jewish religion emphasizes profit and materialism

   [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFoxman201098-133]

5. It is okay for Jews to cheat non-Jews

   [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFoxman2010102-134]

6. Jews use their power to benefit "their own kind"

   [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFoxman2010105-135]

[Gerald Krefetz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerald_Krefetz) summarizes the myth as "\[Jews] control the banks, the money supply, the economy, and businesses—of the community, of the country, of the world".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKrefetz198245-136] Krefetz gives, as illustrations, many slurs and proverbs (in several different languages) which suggest that Jews are stingy, or greedy, or miserly, or aggressive bargainers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKrefetz19826–7-137] During the nineteenth century, Jews were described as "scurrilous, stupid, and tight-fisted", but after the [Jewish Emancipation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_Emancipation) and the rise of Jews to the middle- or upper-class in Europe were portrayed as "clever, devious, and manipulative financiers out to dominate \[world finances]".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKrefetz198247-138]

[Léon Poliakov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L%C3%A9on_Poliakov) asserts that economic antisemitism is not a distinct form of antisemitism, but merely a manifestation of theologic antisemitism (because, without the theological causes of economic antisemitism, there would be no economic antisemitism). In opposition to this view, Derek Penslar contends that in the modern era, economic antisemitism is "distinct and nearly constant" but theological antisemitism is "often subdued".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPenslar200112-139]

An academic study by Francesco D'Acunto, Marcel Prokopczuk, and Michael Weber showed that people who live in areas of Germany that contain the most brutal history of antisemitic persecution are more likely to be distrustful of finance in general. Therefore, they tended to invest less money in the stock market and make poor financial decisions. The study concluded, "that the persecution of minorities reduces not only the long-term wealth of the persecuted but of the persecutors as well."[^cite_note-140]

### Racial antisemitism

Main article: [Racial antisemitism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_antisemitism)



A Jewish Soviet soldier taken prisoner by the German Army, August 1941. At least 50,000 Jewish soldiers were shot after selection.

[^cite_note-141]

Racial antisemitism is prejudice against [Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jew) as a racial/ethnic group, rather than [Judaism](15624.md) as a religion.[^cite_note-142]

Racial antisemitism is the idea that the Jews are a distinct and inferior race compared to their host nations. In the late 19th century and early 20th century, it gained mainstream acceptance as part of the [eugenics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eugenics) movement, which categorized non-Europeans as inferior. It more specifically claimed that Northern Europeans, or "Aryans", were superior. Racial antisemites saw the Jews as part of a Semitic race and emphasized their non-European origins and culture. They saw Jews as beyond redemption even if they converted to the majority religion.[^cite_note-143]

Racial antisemitism replaced the hatred of Judaism with the hatred of Jews as a group. In the context of the [Industrial Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_Revolution), following the [Jewish Emancipation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_Emancipation), Jews rapidly urbanized and experienced a period of greater social mobility. With the decreasing role of religion in public life tempering religious antisemitism, a combination of growing [nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalism), the rise of eugenics, and resentment at the socio-economic success of the Jews led to the newer, and more virulent, racist antisemitism.[^cite_note-144]

In the early 19th century, a number of laws enabling the emancipation of the Jews were enacted in Western European countries.[^cite_note-145][^cite_note-146] The old laws restricting them to [ghettos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghetto), as well as the many laws that limited their property rights, rights of worship and occupation, were rescinded. Despite this, traditional discrimination and hostility to Jews on religious grounds persisted and was supplemented by [racial antisemitism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_antisemitism), encouraged by the work of racial theorists such as [Joseph Arthur de Gobineau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Arthur_de_Gobineau) and particularly his *Essay on the Inequality of the Human Race* of 1853–1855. [Nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalist) agendas based on [ethnicity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnicity), known as [ethnonationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnonationalism), usually excluded the Jews from the national community as an alien race.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBeller200764-147] Allied to this were theories of [Social Darwinism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Darwinism), which stressed a putative conflict between higher and lower races of human beings. Such theories, usually posited by northern Europeans, advocated the superiority of white [Aryans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aryan) to [Semitic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semitic_people) Jews.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBeller200757–59-148]

### Political antisemitism

> The whole problem of the Jews exists only in nation states, for here their energy and higher intelligence, their accumulated capital of spirit and will, gathered from generation to generation through a long schooling in suffering, must become so preponderant as to arouse mass envy and hatred. In almost all contemporary nations, therefore—in direct proportion to the degree to which they act up nationalistically—the literary obscenity of leading the Jews to slaughter as scapegoats of every conceivable public and internal misfortune is spreading.

— [Friedrich Nietzsche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friedrich_Nietzsche), 1886, \[MA 1 475]

[^cite_note-149]

[William Brustein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_I._Brustein) defines political antisemitism as hostility toward Jews based on the belief that Jews seek national or world power. Yisrael Gutman characterizes political antisemitism as tending to "lay responsibility on the Jews for defeats and political economic crises" while seeking to "exploit opposition and resistance to Jewish influence as elements in political party platforms."[^cite_note-150] [Derek J. Penslar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Derek_J._Penslar) wrote, "Political antisemitism identified the Jews as responsible for all the anxiety-provoking social forces that characterized [modernity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modernity)."[^cite_note-151]

According to Viktor Karády, political antisemitism became widespread after the legal emancipation of the Jews and sought to reverse some of the consequences of that emancipation.[^cite_note-152]

### Cultural antisemitism

Louis Harap defines cultural antisemitism as "that species of anti-Semitism that charges the Jews with corrupting a given culture and attempting to supplant or succeeding in supplanting the preferred culture with a uniform, crude, "Jewish" culture."[^cite_note-153] Similarly, [Eric Kandel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eric_Kandel) characterizes cultural antisemitism as being based on the idea of "Jewishness" as a "religious or cultural tradition that is acquired through learning, through distinctive traditions and education." According to Kandel, this form of antisemitism views Jews as possessing "unattractive psychological and social characteristics that are acquired through [acculturation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acculturation)."[^cite_note-Kandel30-154] Niewyk and Nicosia characterize cultural antisemitism as focusing on and condemning "the Jews' aloofness from the societies in which they live."[^cite_note-155] An important feature of cultural antisemitism is that it considers the negative attributes of Judaism to be redeemable by education or by religious conversion.[^cite_note-Kandel3031-156]

### Conspiracy theories

See also: [List of conspiracy theories § Antisemitic conspiracy theories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_conspiracy_theories#Antisemitic_conspiracy_theories)

[Holocaust denial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holocaust_denial) and [Jewish conspiracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_conspiracy) theories are also considered forms of antisemitism.[^cite_note-antisemitic-59][^cite_note-ADL_Denial-157] [Zoological conspiracy theories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zoological_conspiracy_theories) have been propagated by Arab media and Arabic language websites, alleging a "Zionist plot" behind the use of animals to attack civilians or to conduct espionage.[^cite_note-158]

### New antisemitism

Main article: [New antisemitism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_antisemitism)



A sign held at a protest in [Edinburgh](9602.md), Scotland, January 2009

Starting in the 1990s, some scholars have advanced the concept of [new antisemitism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_antisemitism), coming simultaneously from the [left](18499.md), the [right](56522.md), and [radical Islam](15012.md), which tends to focus on opposition to the creation of a Jewish homeland in what is now the State of Israel (formerly Mandatory Palestine),[^cite_note-New-AS-List-159] and they argue that the language of [anti-Zionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Zionism) and [criticism of Israel](30074296.md) are used to attack Jews more broadly. In this view, the proponents of the new concept believe that criticisms of Israel and [Zionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zionism) are often disproportionate in degree and unique in kind, and they attribute this to antisemitism.[^cite_note-JP-26]

Jewish scholar [Gustavo Perednik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gustavo_Perednik) posited in 2004 that anti-Zionism in itself represents a form of discrimination against Jews, in that it singles out Jewish national aspirations as an illegitimate and racist endeavor, and "proposes actions that would result in the death of millions of Jews".[^cite_note-JP-26] Proponents of this theory assert that the new antisemitism deploys traditional antisemitic motifs, including older motifs such as the [blood libel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blood_libel).[^cite_note-New-AS-List-159]

Critics of the concept view it as trivializing the meaning of antisemitism, and as [exploiting antisemitism in order to silence debate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weaponization_of_antisemitism) and to deflect attention from legitimate criticism of the State of Israel, and, by associating anti-Zionism with antisemitism, misusing it to taint anyone opposed to Israeli actions and policies.[^cite_note-160][^cite_note-161]
